### 36. Chainsaw.pdf

**Completed 2026-10-10.** All **20 physical PDF pages** were text-read, rendered and individually inspected, including the screenshot-only command details for IPFS, mail decoding, SSH, listeners and bmap. Extraction yielded 16,453 characters with no warnings. Source: Hack The Box, *Chainsaw*, prepared by MinatoTW, machine authors artikrh and absolutezero, cover dated 8 October 2019, document D19.100.43. SHA-256: `50ad300cd5befc6bcc19b5364cad5105e1b20a040a54501e426cf10be10a50f2`.

| Accepted change | Source pages and useful decision |
|---|---|
| New `ipfs` | 10â€“11 motivate local block inventory, named UnixFS traversal and selected file retrieval. Adds offline repository selection, modern raw-CID inventory differences, pins/MFS as distinct views, incomplete blocks and partial-output handling. |
| New `bmap` | 19â€“20 motivate a narrowly scoped trailing-slack read with Daniel Ridge's legacy tool, retaining original allocation and distinguishing filesystem slack from RAM or ordinary file bytes. Source inspection adds build/mapping limitations, unchecked-I/O caveats and separate binary/log output. |
| Enriched `cast` | 4â€“8 and 13â€“19 provide ABI/getter and state-versus-transaction evidence. Adds Foundry read-only equivalents for runtime code and a getter at a selected block, with return-type decoding and caller/historical-state conditions. The PDF uses Web3.py; Cast is an explicitly verified adaptation to the existing package. |
| Enriched `base64` | 10â€“12 motivate exact MIME-part extraction, transfer encoding versus encrypted key content, and avoiding a second decode after a MIME parser. |

README catalogue entries were updated. Both new packages contain only their own SKILL.md and have no shared runtime dependency. Existing SSH forwarding, John key-format handling and scan guidance already cover their corresponding tasks; no redundant examples or separate Web3 SDK package were added.

**Page coverage and source qualifications.** Pages 1â€“2 introduce the two Ethereum-backed applications, IPFS and filesystem slack. Page 3 shows an all-TCP scan followed by selected-port service/default-script probing. Anonymous FTP returns Solidity source, a JSON artifact and an address file; an unclassified port is not itself proof of Ethereum. The existing scan guidance already handles scope and uncertain service classification.

Pages 4â€“5 show a string getter and setter and their ABI descriptions. An ABI specifies encoding/decoding; a matching filename does not prove that it describes deployed bytecode at a particular address. Pages 6â€“7 construct a Web3.py client, select the first exposed account and read a getter. Client construction does not load or verify every storage value, and an account in an RPC list is not proof of an unlocked signer. The old camelCase API is generation-specific; it was not copied into a new Python skill. The Cast adaptation checks the chosen chain/address/block and separates decode or missing-state errors from valid values.

Page 8 submits a transaction, reads the updated string and shows two ICMP request/reply pairs in tcpdump. The changed value and observed traffic are separate evidence stages; a submitted transaction hash is not by itself a mined successful receipt, and a getter does not prove an operating-system consumer executed the returned text. The historical local chain may mine immediately; portable guidance must not assume that timing. Page 9 shows a reverse-shell identity and a passwd excerpt. Contrary to the prose claiming only bobby has a Bash shell, the screenshot also assigns `/bin/bash` to administrator. Account shell metadata is separate from current SSH access policy. No payload or network probe was executed.

Page 10 enumerates the local IPFS store, loops over CIDs with `ipfs ls` and suppresses errors. IPFS does not require a blockchain, contrary to the source's description. The historical `Qm...` inventory representation differs from current Kubo's raw-codec CIDv1 inventory; not every stored block is a complete named file or UnixFS directory. The new guidance retains errors and obtains correctly typed root CIDs from known references/pins/MFS rather than blindly replaying the loop. A missing HTTP gateway does not by itself establish that all CLI operations stay offline.

Page 11 shows a multipart email's header excerpt, followed by a claimed extracted attachment decode and SSH-key recovery. The displayed header excerpt is insufficient to reproduce the exact attachment extraction: the part body and relevant part headers are omitted. `X-Attached` and `.enc` do not establish Content-Transfer-Encoding or encryption. Base64 decoding is separate from parsing/decrypting the resulting key; MIME tools may already have decoded it. The John output warns of possible candidate false positives. Page 12 then shows a distinct successful SSH identity observation; no recovered secret or authentication was reused here.

Page 13 shows a root-owned SUID executable and contract source containing application state. A failed password search does not establish that a hash cannot be cracked. Pages 14â€“15 show an application credential rejection, a generated address file, a loopback listener, SSH local forwarding and SCP. The unprivileged netstat output explicitly lacks some process attribution. A listening socket and a functioning forward do not independently establish the application protocol; the existing SSH package already explains both ends and destination failures.

Page 16 hardcodes a different contract address from the address displayed on page 14, despite prose saying it came from that file. Portable instructions use the actual deployment evidence. Pages 16â€“17 change application credentials and then receive an approval failure. Page 18 changes approval and receives an insufficient-funds message; these responses describe different application checks, not a universal login model. Pages 18â€“19 show a transfer predicate requiring a positive amount no greater than the remaining totalSupply. The page 19 explanation reverses the comparison in prose. Its contract balance is application state, not automatically the account's native-currency balance, and rerunning the same transfer after supply changes can behave differently. The later root `id` is an independent observed outcome of the source's SUID program. No contract transaction, financial transfer or SUID execution was performed.

Pages 19â€“20 compare a logical file read with `bmap --mode slack`. The shown file size is 52 bytes, filesystem block size 4096 and trailing slack 4044. This concerns bytes after EOF in an allocated filesystem block, not slack in RAM. Copying the logical file normally does not retain that original allocation/tail. The skill's arithmetic example is labelled illustrative and does not claim a recovered artifact. The source's final fragment is a finding within that lab, not a generally valid interpretation of any readable slack bytes.

**Primary verification, reviewed 2026-10-10.** The [Kubo CLI reference](https://docs.ipfs.tech/reference/kubo/cli/) verifies offline mode, timeouts, local inventory, recursive-pin listing, MFS listing/stat, UnixFS ls and cat byte limits. The [refs source](https://github.com/ipfs/kubo/blob/master/core/commands/refs.go) and CLI notes qualify raw block inventory and possible online retrieval. [Basic CLI operations](https://docs.ipfs.tech/how-to/kubo-basic-cli/) distinguishes directories and files; [IPFS concepts](https://docs.ipfs.tech/concepts/what-is-ipfs/) supplies the content-addressed model. No repository was initialized, migrated, locked, pinned, fetched or garbage-collected during review.

Daniel Ridge's [bmap source copy](https://github.com/CameronLonsdale/bmap) records its legacy provenance and a 2017 architecture/alignment patch. Its [bmap.c](https://github.com/CameronLonsdale/bmap/blob/master/bmap.c) verifies the read-only raw-device open for slack mode, regular-file check, terminal-block calculation, source-mutating alternate modes, append behavior of `--outfile` and unchecked raw I/O/exit-status limitations. The [logger](https://github.com/CameronLonsdale/bmap/blob/master/mft/log.c) writes diagnostics to stderr. Consequently the skill separates raw stdout from diagnostics and does not treat successful status or output length as complete validation. No old binary was compiled or run, and no filesystem/device compatibility is claimed.

Foundry's [call reference](https://getfoundry.sh/reference/cast/call/) and [code reference](https://getfoundry.sh/reference/cast/code/) were retrieved directly and their CLI help text inspected after the web reader failed on those pages. They verify non-publishing call simulation, explicit block selection, signature/argument syntax and runtime bytecode lookup. The [Ethereum JSON-RPC specification](https://ethereum.org/en/developers/docs/apis/json-rpc/) distinguishes calls, account lists, code, transaction submission and receipts. [RFC 2045](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2045) and [RFC 2046](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2046) support per-part transfer encoding and multipart boundaries. Primary source snapshots and hashes are saved with the checks in ignored work.

**Checks actually run.** All four focused skill validators passed. Repository checks passed for **152 packages, 191 Markdown files, 239 local links and 345 Bash blocks**, syntax only. Catalogue membership matches all 152 folders. Final read-failure branches preserve a nonzero shell result and were rechecked after editing. Final documentation/link checks and `git diff --check` passed in the explicit repository context. No packaging/checker implementation changed.

The selected tool names ipfs, bmap, cast and base64 were absent from the Windows process PATH. No affected CLI runtime fixture was executed, including through a separate Git Bash tool directory. No source mail/key/repository/device, blockchain endpoint, capture, credential recovery, target authentication, Linux execution or worker evaluation was used. All added command behavior is documentation/source-reviewed and Bash-syntax-checked only. Raw text, all renders, before snapshots, source snapshots, review diff and structural results remain in ignored work. Deliberately deferred material: on-chain setters and application-state manipulation, command injection/reverse shell, password recovery/use, target SSH/SCP, SUID execution, source-device acquisition and any slack-writing modes. Original PDFs and unrelated methodologies/edits were preserved; no installation, system/configuration change, deletion, commit or push occurred.


### 37. Chaos.pdf

**Completed 2026-10-10.** All **19 physical PDF pages** were text-read, rendered and individually inspected, including the screenshots omitted from extraction. Extraction produced 12,329 characters without warnings. Source: Hack The Box, *Chaos*, prepared by MinatoTW, machine author felamos, cover dated 25 April 2019, document D19.100.18. SHA-256: `58bf8604298ef26b3bc848790036eceb983a7fe68c2b74c9094cd9373c9d5cf9`.

| Accepted change | Source pages and useful decision |
|---|---|
| New `firefox-decrypt` | 17â€“18 motivate explicit offline profile selection, matching key/login files, known Primary Password input and private JSON export. Adds NSS/version failures, skipped records, secret-bearing debug output and historical-record versus current-access interpretation. |
| Enriched `openssl` | 3, 7â€“8 motivate a skill-local IMAP/TLS reference distinguishing transport, protocol and mailbox state, including read-only EXAMINE and PEEK. Pages 8â€“11 extend the existing CBC reference for a custom length/IV header, SHA-256-derived AES-256 key and exact removal of space padding. OpenSSL is an explicit adaptation of the PDF's Python CBC example. |
| Enriched `bash` | 15â€“16 distinguish restricted mode, lookup failures and a rejected command-not-found handler; a child shell, PATH and effective identity remain separate observations. |
| Enriched `wpscan` | 5â€“6 qualify explicit user-ID enumeration and author-name evidence, and distinguish a protected post's password from WordPress account authentication. |

The README catalogue was updated to 153 independently installable skills. The new package contains only SKILL.md; both OpenSSL references remain inside that existing package. Existing nmap, curl, gobuster, base64 and archive guidance already cover the corresponding discovery/transport/extraction decisions, so no duplicate examples were added. No separate TeX or firepwd package was created from an exploit example or a tool name alone.

**Page coverage and source qualifications.** Pages 1â€“2 identify the case and summarize its discovery, mail, custom encryption, TeX, restricted-shell and browser-profile stages. Page 3 shows the all-TCP scan followed by detailed service probing. IMAP/POP3 on plaintext and implicit-TLS ports do not prove two independent instances of each service. Page 4's different HTTP responses by hostname motivate virtual-host selection; the existing curl guidance already supports this without changing the local hosts file. Page 5 uses Gobuster v2.0.1 syntax, and its 301 response needs follow-up before identifying the actual WordPress installation path.

Page 6 uses bare WPScan -e and describes it as all-plugin/user enumeration. The current source defines a broader default collection but not universal discovery of all plugins; the existing skill already corrects that ambiguity. The screenshot identifies an author/user name by multiple methods, then separately shows a password-protected post opening. That content password is not proof of account login or administrator access. Case credentials were not reused or copied into runtime instructions.

Page 7's command says port 995, whereas page 3 classifies 995 as POP3 and 993 as IMAP; the following screenshot is clearly an IMAP conversation. This internal inconsistency cannot establish a special port mapping. The new reference requires the actual endpoint/greeting and distinguishes 993 implicit TLS from IMAP STARTTLS. The source's SELECT opens read-write, and BODY[TEXT] can affect Seen state. The portable inspection adaptation uses EXAMINE and BODY.PEEK, preserves UID context and waits for tagged completion. The source's shell-style trailing comments are annotations, not valid generic protocol input. An empty INBOX does not cover the Drafts folder. Page 8 shows MIME part headers, but not the complete two encoded attachment bodies; exact source-file recovery cannot be reproduced from that excerpt.

Pages 8â€“10 describe a 16-character zero-filled decimal byte count followed by a 16-byte IV and CBC ciphertext. The code uses a 65,536-byte read buffer, despite page 9 incorrectly describing 64*1024 bits as 16 bytes. Padding applies when the chunk length is not a multiple of 16, not merely when a chunk is shorter than 16. The source's SHA-256 digest is a 32-byte AES key. The original length is significant for removing space padding; calling it insignificant on page 10 loses that information. The decryption listing's text-mode open is not suitable for Python 3 binary ciphertext. A missing IV does not universally prevent recovery of every CBC block: later blocks depend on the preceding ciphertext. The added interpretation reflects that limit without treating unauthenticated output as trustworthy.

Page 11 prints readable inner base64 output followed by invalid input. Space padding plausibly explains the source symptom, but the supplied encrypted attachment is absent and was not reconstructed. The accepted workflow validates the established envelope, retains exactly the recorded original bytes, then checks the inner decoder's status. A visible plaintext prefix is not complete success. The local synthetic tests below independently verified the partial-output behavior; they do not reproduce the source artifact.

Pages 12â€“14 show a PDF web form, JavaScript/Ajax request, pdfTeX log, blacklist rejection, a different execution primitive and a shell. The log explicitly shows write18 enabled. This is evidence about the observed engine configuration and wrapper, not proof that every LaTeX package permits command execution. The page 14 log includes an executed identity before a later fatal PDF-generation error; failed final output does not undo an earlier side effect. No payload, compiler, network listener or shell callback was executed during ingestion.

Pages 15â€“16 show restricted Bash rejecting cd and a slash-containing command-not-found handler. A later diagnostic explicitly says ls is absent from PATH; this is more precise than saying ls itself is restricted. The tar checkpoint creates a child shell in the source and does not itself change the user identity. PATH remains limited, and a subsequently started shell presents another PATH value; interpreter startup must be checked independently. The new Bash addition teaches diagnosis, not the escape payload. Existing tar extraction scope was left intact.

Page 17's ZIP capture reports unreadable/skipped files, and displayed source/destination sizes differ. The transfer is therefore not demonstrated to be a complete byte-identical profile acquisition. The source still shows an index and one profile directory. Page 18 uses a known profile password and outputs a saved Webmin login; that historical site record alone does not establish an OS root credential or current validity. The subsequent su session is separate evidence. Page 19 shows the Webmin command-shell interface and output, whose availability depends on the authenticated account's rights. No browser profile was opened or decrypted here and no recovered credential was tested.

**Primary verification, reviewed 2026-10-10.** [Firefox Decrypt usage](https://github.com/unode/firefox_decrypt) documents known-password recovery, formats and Firefox 144+/NSS compatibility. The directly retrieved [CLI source](https://github.com/unode/firefox_decrypt/blob/main/firefox_decrypt.py) verifies explicit paths, list/choice handling, loading NSS before listing, direct profile fallback, stdin input, JSON fields, error branches and debug-secret exposure. Its JSON reader can skip incomplete records and some decode exceptions produce placeholders; status zero does not imply every record was recovered. The fetched source is 1,234 lines and differs from the older indexed web rendering; no downloaded source was imported or executed. [Mozilla profile documentation](https://support.mozilla.org/en-US/kb/profiles-where-firefox-stores-user-data) identifies key4.db/logins.json. Source snapshot hashes are saved in ignored work.

The [OpenSSL s_client manual](https://docs.openssl.org/3.5/man1/openssl-s_client/) verifies SNI, hostname/trust enforcement, STARTTLS, CRLF and quiet/EOF handling. [RFC 9051](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9051.html) supplies mailbox state, tags, UIDVALIDITY, literal framing and read-only/PEEK behavior; [RFC 8314](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8314.html) confirms mail-TLS conventions. The [enc manual](https://docs.openssl.org/3.5/man1/openssl-enc/), [PyCryptodome CBC documentation](https://www.pycryptodome.org/src/cipher/classic), [Python digest API](https://docs.python.org/3/library/hashlib.html) and the existing NIST reference were used to separate explicit key bytes, block size, producer padding and CBC's lack of authentication.

The upstream-authored [Bash manual](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man1/bash.1.html#RESTRICTED_SHELL) was reviewed through its man7 rendering after GNU web fetches failed. [WPScan enumeration source](https://github.com/wpscanteam/wpscan/blob/master/app/controllers/enumeration/cli_options.rb) confirms explicit user ranges and default sets; [WordPress content visibility](https://wordpress.org/documentation/article/content-visibility-classic-editor/) distinguishes protected content. Earlier WPScan history was moved out of the runtime footer: documentation review 2026-10-09, Linux execution untested, BigBang p. 3; original sources were [upstream usage](https://github.com/wpscanteam/wpscan/blob/master/README.md), the enumeration source and [packaged help](https://www.kali.org/tools/wpscan/). That historical status is preserved without claiming an additional current runtime test.

**Checks actually run.** All four focused validators passed. Repository checks passed for **153 packages, 193 Markdown files, 241 local links and 350 Bash blocks**, syntax only. Catalogue membership matches all skill folders. Final link checks and git diff --check passed; source snapshots, before snapshots, all renders and the review diff remain in ignored work.

Ten inert Windows checks passed: five public-fixture CBC round trips (trailing spaces, aligned input, a 65,536-byte boundary, inner base64 and empty input), GNU base64 failure with partial output versus exact-length success, wrong-IV zero status with later blocks intact, truncated-block rejection, and two builtin-only Bash mode checks. Versions: Python 3.12.10, OpenSSL 3.5.7, GNU coreutils base64 8.32 and Git Bash 5.3.15. Fixtures and raw results are under ignored runs/overnight-chaos-2026-10-10. They validate these byte/mode decisions on Windows, not the PDF's original Python code, an IMAP service, NSS recovery, WPScan, Linux or an agent's investigation outcome. The selected Firefox Decrypt/WPScan executables were absent from PATH.

Deliberately deferred material: source attachment/credential recovery, mailbox authentication or acquisition, browser-profile transfer/decryption, password guessing/reuse, hosts-file changes, TeX injection and reverse shell, tar-based restriction bypass, su and Webmin access. No source secret, target, installation, system/configuration change, original-PDF deletion/move, commit or push was used. Unrelated edits and assessment methodologies were preserved.


### 38. Checker.pdf

**Completed 2026-10-10.** All **18 physical PDF pages** were text-read, rendered and individually visually inspected, including commands and results present only in screenshots. Extraction produced 24,944 characters without warnings. Source: Hack The Box, *Checker*, prepared by dotguy, machine author 0xyassine, cover dated 28 May 2025. SHA-256: `9f0c2c114cd4b17c7012dab4b747e1fc0aeab31822483f080c15477f865935d3`.

| Accepted change | Source pages and useful decision |
|---|---|
| New `cp` | Page 10's two backup scripts motivate deliberate directory nesting, private destination parents, preservation versus explicit non-preservation and verification of bytes separately from metadata. Corrects the unsupported generalization that recursive copying alone broadens permissions. |
| New `oathtool` | Pages 10â€“11 motivate known-secret TOTP with explicit encoding/parameters, private stdin input, leading-zero handling and clock-versus-enrollment diagnosis. Adds a published public reference vector, without claiming local execution. |
| New `ipcs` | Pages 14â€“17 motivate read-only inspection of System V metadata. This is an explicit diagnostic adaptation: the PDF uses shmget/shmat in C, not the ipcs CLI. Connects a logged key to an observed shmid, permission bits, creator/owner/PIDs and namespace limits. |
| Enriched `mysql` | Page 17 motivates distinguishing mysql client shell commands from server SQL/file privileges, and checking version-specific client-command controls before altering an integration. |

The README catalogue now contains 156 independently installable skills. Each new package contains only SKILL.md. Existing nmap, curl, base64, hashcat, bash, sudo and Python guidance covers the corresponding discovery, request encoding, recovery-result and wrapper decisions without additional repetition. Ghidra appears as a GUI decompiler; that alone does not support a new CLI skill. No Teampass/BookStack exploit wrapper or shared-memory attack helper was packaged.

**Page coverage and source qualifications.** Page 1 describes the Teampass, BookStack, SSH/TOTP and privileged checker stages. Page 2's scan header names heal.htb despite the Checker context; retain endpoint ambiguity instead of copying that name. The script's suggestion that an HTTP service might redirect does not prove an open proxy. Page 3's hosts-file example contains backticks inside double quotes, which would be shell substitution if pasted into Bash. No hosts-file mutation was made. A version-looking asset URL is a lead, not proof of the installed vulnerable build or authenticated exploit prerequisites.

Page 4 shows a Teampass SQL-injection PoC extracting two bcrypt strings, Hashcat mode 3200 recovering one and a separate successful web login. Existing hashcat guidance already distinguishes input records, recovered candidates and authentication. Page 5's stored service/SSH credentials and subsequent verification-code prompt do not alone establish a completed SSH login. No case credential or OTP seed was copied to runtime examples or used.

Pages 6â€“7 alter a PHP filter-chain oracle request: nested base64 data is inserted in an image/body and a mapping is replaced by raw joined form fields. That construction does not generally preserve plus signs, ampersands or equals signs. The source's earlier POST description differs from its PUT implementation; page 8's captured save-draft request is PUT with JSON, whereas the later adaptation uses form encoding. A current method, body format, endpoint, session and CSRF value must be coherent. Creating a book/draft is a remote write, not passive request capture.

Page 9's command arguments and echoed headers show different CSRF/cookie values, so they are not a single literal reproducible session. The displayed passwd read is partial. Failure to retrieve the original authenticator path on page 10 does not prove that the file is universally absent or unprotected. The successful backup-path result establishes a different location in this case.

The page 10 screenshots explicitly contrast recursive copying with `--remove-destination -p` against `--remove-destination --no-preserve=mode,ownership`. The latter deliberately resets mode/ownership behavior; it is not equivalent to plain cp -r. Existing versus newly recreated destinations, umask, ACLs and parent-directory access matter. The new guidance uses a fresh private outer directory and archive preservation, then checks selected file metadata separately from bytes. Archive mode is not a consistent live-application snapshot and can suppress some extended-attribute/context preservation failures. Copying source/. can replace destination-directory metadata, so the private outer layer is retained when containment matters.

Pages 10â€“11 show a Base32 seed followed by a TOTP_AUTH configuration line, and then oathtool output with a leading zero. Only the seed is key input; the complete configuration file is not a Base32 key. Page 10's prose misspells the utility name, while page 11 shows oathtool. Code generation and the later successful SSH identity are separate evidence. The accepted skill requires known provisioning parameters and avoids secrets in arguments or verbose logs.

Page 11's sudo rule and Bash wrapper lead into page 12's checker outcomes. Sourcing .env executes shell code; a C getenv call additionally requires the relevant variables to be exported. The wrapper's filtered username does not establish trust in later data. A match against a particular leaked-hash list does not establish comprehensive password safety when no match is found. The HTTP server exposes its served directory, including configuration and hash files in the listing. No transfer-integrity hash is shown. These conditions are recorded without reproducing the service or transferring files.

Page 13's decompiled main obtains environment variables and enforces a maximum strlen of 0x14 bytes, not a universal twenty-character Unicode rule. Its database lookup fetches a password-hash field; a null result's meaning depends on omitted error handling. Page 14's main writes the shared-memory value, flushes output, sleeps, then calls notification and cleanup. This contradicts page 16's prose placing the write after the sleep. The source's 0x400 request is 1,024 bytes. The first shmget argument is a key; its return is the segment ID. Linux flag value 0x3b6 separates into IPC_CREAT and permissions 0666, not a sticky-bit file mode. IPC_EXCL is absent, so source alone does not prove that a fresh segment was created. ipcs provides a bounded way to inspect actual metadata; it was not run here.

Page 15 writes a timestamp/hash string, then reads and searches delimiters. The `+1` after the delimiter pointer already skips that delimiter before the trim helper; attributing the entire operation to trimming is inaccurate. Pages 16â€“17 contain an attack helper that changes shared memory and appends unchecked data. Its shmget flags do not reassign an existing segment's ownership or permissions. The helper was not compiled or executed. The subsequently constructed mysql invocation crosses OS-shell, client-command and SQL parsing layers; the system command runs under the client process on the client host, independently of server FILE privilege. Filtering the wrapper's username cannot sanitize the later shared-memory input.

Page 18 redirects output to a path called pipe, but does not demonstrate that the path is a FIFO. Buffering and scheduling prevent treating the displayed sequence as a reliable universal race recipe. The displayed SUID file mode and a later process's effective UID are separate observations. The May 31 file timestamp differs from the May 24 shared-memory display and May 28 cover date; the screenshots should not be treated as one verified timeline. No race, shell payload, permission change or privileged execution was performed.

**Primary verification, reviewed 2026-10-10.** The [OATH Toolkit manual](https://oath-toolkit.codeberg.page/oathtool.1.html) verifies stdin/file key input, Base32 versus hexadecimal, HOTP/TOTP modes, algorithms, time parameters and verbose-secret exposure. [RFC 6238 Appendix B](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6238.html#appendix-B) supplies the public SHA1 test vector (59 seconds, eight digits, expected 94287082); its clock/step requirements support the mismatch decision. This is a published reference value, not a locally captured result.

The upstream-authored [ipcs manual](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man1/ipcs.1.html) verifies resource/ID selection, mutually exclusive display formats and procfs fallback visibility. The [shmget manual](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/shmget.2.html), [Linux IPC flag header](https://github.com/torvalds/linux/blob/master/include/uapi/linux/ipc.h), [shmat/shmdt manual](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/shmat.2.html) and [IPC namespace manual](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man7/ipc_namespaces.7.html) establish key/ID separation, creation versus permission bits, existing-segment behavior, attach/detach PID semantics and namespace scope. Metadata is not segment-content or last-writer evidence.

GNU's web manual could not be fetched directly. The [upstream coreutils manual source](https://github.com/coreutils/coreutils/blob/master/doc/coreutils.texi), saved under ignored work, and the upstream [cp option parser](https://github.com/coreutils/coreutils/blob/master/src/cp.c) / [copy implementation](https://github.com/coreutils/coreutils/blob/master/src/copy.c) verify preservation limits, destination removal and explicit non-preservation. The copy implementation applies default regular-file read/write permissions filtered by umask for newly created destinations under explicit no-preserve-mode. The manual distinguishes this from ordinary source-mode creation and existing destination behavior. No downloaded source was built or executed.

The [MySQL client-command manual](https://dev.mysql.com/doc/refman/8.4/en/mysql-commands.html) and [client options](https://dev.mysql.com/doc/refman/8.4/en/mysql-command-options.html) verify the local system command, formatting flags, noninteractive binary-mode exceptions and the 8.4.3/8.4.6 control additions. These version-specific controls are not assumed for MariaDB or historical clients; disabling client commands does not make arbitrary SQL harmless.

**Checks actually run.** Focused validators passed for cp, ipcs, oathtool and mysql. The repository checker passed for **156 skill packages, 196 Markdown files, 244 local links and 356 Bash blocks**, with Bash parsing only. Catalogue membership matches the skill folders; git diff --check passed. The final content diff was reviewed, including the correction that a source/. archive copy can change destination-directory metadata. The affected oathtool, ipcs and mysql executables were absent from PATH. No affected CLI fixture, copied-file permission fixture, TOTP calculation, database query, IPC inspection, Linux execution or worker evaluation occurred. Windows structural checks do not establish those behaviors. Artifacts are in ignored `work/overnight-ingest-2026-10-10/Checker/`: text, page counts, all 18 renders, source snapshot, check results and review diff.

Deliberately deferred material: vulnerability reproduction, password/hash recovery, secret acquisition/use, service or SSH authentication, backup/script/system changes, binary execution/decompilation beyond the printed evidence, shared-memory attachment/mutation, race timing, mysql shell payloads and privilege escalation. No target, installation, original-PDF deletion/move, commit or push was used. Unrelated edits and assessment methodologies were preserved.


### 39. Chemistry.pdf

**Completed 2026-10-10.** All **eight physical PDF pages** were text-read, rendered and individually visually inspected, including screenshot-only interface details. Extraction produced 10,544 characters without warnings. Source: Hack The Box, *Chemistry*, prepared by kavigihan, machine author FisMatHack, cover dated 6 March 2025, document D25.100.325. SHA-256: `bf350df894cea940580a13f62829f15da74c52a278c333a71a8e24f2253a249a`.

| Accepted change | Source pages and useful decision |
|---|---|
| Enriched `curl` | Pages 7â€“8 motivate a concrete normalized-versus-literal request-path example and preserving status, headers and body from one GET. Clarifies why an HTTP 200 or a partially received body is insufficient disclosure evidence. |
| Enriched `feroxbuster` | Pages 6â€“7 distinguish a denied directory index from accessible known children and qualify static-route/version inference. |
| Enriched `python3` | Pages 1, 3â€“4 motivate tracing input into a called library parser and rejecting an empty/restricted builtins environment as proof of a safe evaluator. |
| Enriched `script` | Page 4 motivates explicit command-versus-transcript argument roles, correcting a reversed source invocation. Earlier Altered source/review history was moved here. |

No new package was warranted. The catalogue remains 156 independently installable skills; the Feroxbuster catalogue scope was refreshed. Existing nmap, sqlite3, hashcat, ss, ssh and ssh-keygen guidance already supports the scan coverage, structured record export, hash-mode/identity, socket ownership, forwarding and key-file decisions. No standalone pymatgen or aiohttp exploit skill was created from a library vulnerability demonstration.

**Page coverage and source qualifications.** Page 1 introduces two historical library vulnerabilities and a two-stage Nmap command. Its first scan does not request every TCP port; two displayed open services therefore do not establish complete TCP/UDP coverage. Parsing human-oriented scan output with grep/cut also needs a valid nonempty port list before the second scan. Existing nmap guidance covers these limits.

Page 2 identifies observed SSH/Werkzeug banners and shows registration/login followed by the CIF upload dashboard. Registration and upload mutate the application; a web-server banner does not identify the exact installed pymatgen dependency. Page 3's vulnerability screenshot and adapted CIF place an expression in a crystallographic transformation field. The root cause is unsafe evaluation inside a parser, not a rule that all text-based CIF files or all deserializers execute code. The accepted Python addition traces the actual library call and deployed version without parsing the suspect file.

Page 3's shell payload uses callback port 9000, while page 4's listener is on 9090. No intervening port mapping or correction is shown, so the transcript is not directly reproducible as printed. The payload is fetched and piped to a shell in the source; neither that command nor the parser, HTTP server, listener or callback was executed here. The page 4 uploaded-file screenshot shows a View action and the narrative associates that with parsing; merely accepting an upload does not independently establish execution.

Page 4 prints `script -c /dev/null bash`, reversing the intended roles for util-linux script: /dev/null becomes command text and bash becomes the transcript filename. The displayed prompt alone does not validate that invocation or complete terminal behavior. The existing skill already has the correct command and now makes argument roles explicit. The source's SQLite query requests every column from a user table and its continuation on page 5 contains fourteen records. The database file itself is not supplied. Existing sqlite3 guidance prefers read-only working copies, schema inspection and bounded structured output; splitting default output on a delimiter can corrupt fields or lose account association.

Page 5 shows four recovered candidates with Hashcat mode 0, an account-file grep and an SSH invocation followed by a rosa prompt for socket inspection. The hash display's length alone does not prove raw MD5, and matching a database hash is separate from current SSH acceptance. Filtering passwd for /bin/bash excludes other valid shell configurations. The ss display's Local Address column establishes a 127.0.0.1:8080 listener; the peer wildcard does not make it externally bound. Missing process details do not identify the owner. The forwarding command's destination loopback is resolved on the SSH server; current ssh/ss skills already cover binding, forwarding policy and application checks.

Page 6 claims authentication to the forwarded monitoring application and shows its dashboard, followed by an aiohttp 3.9.1 banner. The later Feroxbuster/curl commands do not show session credentials, so one cannot infer that each reused the browser's authenticated session or that all routes require authentication. The maintainer advisory requires a particular static-route configuration for CVE-2024-23334; a banner alone is insufficient. The scan reports automatic 404-like filtering and individual CSS resources, while /assets returns 403. Page 7 continues with accessible JavaScript children and denied parent paths. These results are compatible with disabled directory indexes; they do not establish the filesystem root or a vulnerability by themselves. Recheck a known child and a control under the same request context before changing filters or expanding discovery.

Page 7's displayed Bash conditional contains a Markdown-link-style suffix after the closing bracket. The defect is visible in the rendered PDF, not solely text extraction. It is not a directly executable Bash example as printed. Its loop discards one response body while reading status, then makes a second request for content. That pairs evidence from different transactions. The added curl guidance captures all response evidence in one fetch and explains the outgoing path change caused by --path-as-is. The source's three-ancestor success is case-specific and does not reveal a universal static-directory depth.

Pages 7â€“8 show a flag-like body and a truncated private-key block from paths outside the web root. Those displays support the source's claimed read primitive but do not independently identify the serving process as root or demonstrate shell execution. Page 8 ends with key permissions and an SSH command; it provides no resulting authenticated identity output. The incomplete key cannot be validated or used as a complete artifact, and no credential acquisition or login was attempted during ingestion.

**Primary verification, reviewed 2026-10-10.** The [curl option manual](https://curl.se/docs/manpage.html) verifies path normalization control, output/header capture, status reporting and transfer exit semantics. The [aiohttp static-file documentation](https://docs.aiohttp.org/en/stable/web_advanced.html#static-file-handling) confirms default directory-index denial, separate file serving and the significance of follow_symlinks. The [maintainer advisory](https://github.com/aio-libs/aiohttp/security/advisories/GHSA-5h86-8mv2-jq9f) identifies the historical follow_symlinks=True condition, including cases without actual symlinks, and the 3.9.2 fix. No configuration change was applied.

The [pymatgen maintainer advisory](https://github.com/materialsproject/pymatgen/security/advisories/GHSA-vgv8-5cpj-qj2f) identifies JonesFaithfulTransformation.from_transformation_str and its unsafe eval despite __builtins__ being None. [Python's eval documentation](https://docs.python.org/3.12/library/functions.html#eval) confirms code execution and globals/builtins behavior. This supports source inspection rather than running a malicious CIF to establish a version finding. The upstream-authored [script manual](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man1/script.1.html) verifies command/transcript roles, child-status propagation and logging semantics. Relocated script history: documentation reviewed 2026-10-09, Linux execution untested, Altered p. 18, original source [util-linux script manual](https://github.com/util-linux/util-linux/blob/master/term-utils/script.1.adoc). No historical test result was upgraded.

**Checks actually run.** Four inert **Windows curl 8.21.0** fixtures passed against a temporary loopback-only Python 3.12.10 HTTP server. The handler returned synthetic path/sequence markers and never read files. Default normalization sent /probe.txt; --path-as-is retained /docs/../probe.txt. Each capture made exactly one request and paired its headers/body/status. A complete HTTP 500 returned curl status 0; an intentionally short body retained HTTP 200 and partial bytes but returned curl status 18. The server was closed after testing. This verifies client request/capture behavior, not aiohttp routing, a vulnerable parser or the source target. Raw results are in ignored `runs/overnight-chemistry-2026-10-10/`.

Focused validators passed for curl, feroxbuster, python3 and script. The repository checker passed for **156 packages, 196 Markdown files, 244 local links and 356 Bash blocks**; Bash was parsed only. Catalogue membership and git diff --check passed, and the complete changed-content diff was reviewed. Feroxbuster and util-linux script were absent from PATH. No PTY/scanner/library runtime fixture, source database/hash recovery, target connection, Linux execution or worker evaluation occurred. The ignored per-file work directory retains extraction/page counts, all eight renders, relocated history, checks and review diff.

Deliberately deferred material: application registration/upload, malicious CIF construction/parsing, shell payload hosting or execution, listener/callback/TTY session, source database acquisition, password recovery/reuse, SSH forwarding or login, traversal against a real service, sensitive-file/key acquisition and privilege escalation. No tool installation, system/configuration change, original-PDF deletion/move, commit or push occurred. Unrelated edits and methodologies were preserved.


### 40. Cicada.pdf

**Completed 2026-10-10.** All **11 physical PDF pages** were text-read, rendered and individually visually inspected, including the account names, command options and partial failure output. Extraction produced 23,970 characters without warnings. Source: Hack The Box, *Cicada*, prepared by Pho3, machine author theblxckcicada, cover dated 17 January 2025, document D25.100.319. SHA-256: `de3b893fbed89d19b7b6ad2e0d47008f116dba5c355dd0ce33c32a50e8883740`.

| Accepted change | Source pages and useful decision |
|---|---|
| New `impacket-lookupsid` | Pages 5â€“6 support bounded RID lookup, explicit authentication identity, retaining SID scope and principal types, offline filtering and incomplete/error interpretation. |
| Enriched `netexec` | Pages 6â€“8 motivate a current selected-user metadata query and separating description exposure, password validity and DC-local bad-password observations. Existing pairing/stop-on-success guidance already covers the source's spray limitations. |
| Enriched `smbclient` | Pages 3â€“4 and 8 distinguish explicit guest, anonymous and default client identity; prior NetExec authentication is not inherited by a separate SMB client. Earlier source/review history was relocated here. |
| Enriched `evil-winrm` | Pages 9â€“11 motivate current account-SID/token-privilege inspection and the API conditions on backup-privilege access. Earlier source/review history was relocated here. |

The catalogue now has 157 independent skills. Existing nmap and impacket-secretsdump guidance already covers scan completeness, source-store identity, paired hive prerequisites, NT-hash field selection and partial extraction. No duplicate whoami, native reg.exe or PowerShell package was created solely for this short Windows-side sequence. The Linux WinRM client skill contains the relevant remote token inspection.

**Page coverage and source qualifications.** Page 1 outlines the historical lab chain from AD/SMB discovery through credentials exposed in files/metadata, backup privileges and hash-based authentication. This is source narrative, not authorization to reproduce that chain. Page 2 shows Nmap 7.94SVN with -sC -sV -Pn, eleven open TCP ports and 989 filtered ports. The command does not request all TCP ports or UDP. DNS, LDAP, Kerberos and SMB observations support the AD hypothesis; they do not prove an exhaustive service inventory. The odd domain suffix in one LDAP banner should be reconciled with certificate/service names rather than copied blindly. TLS randomness explicitly does not represent time. Later WinRM access is not independently established by the displayed scan.

Page 3 appends a hosts entry and says there is no web interface, a conclusion wider than the shown scan proves. Its first CrackMapExec share request fails with STATUS_USER_SESSION_DELETED; an explicit guest/empty-password request then succeeds and lists HR and IPC$ as readable. These are distinct authentication contexts. Required SMB signing does not itself prevent permitted guest or named-account authentication. Share access labels are separate from permission to read a particular file.

Page 4's prose calls the smbclient connection guest, but its actual command omits -U and the password prompt identifies WORKGROUP\phoe. The transcript alone cannot establish which server-side identity was granted; mapping to guest or omitted context remains possible. The accepted addition makes the requested identity explicit. A quoted file name with spaces is downloaded, with a reported 1,266-byte size. The onboarding template contains an initial-password claim, not proof that every current user still accepts it. No source secret is copied into runtime examples.

Page 5 shows Impacket 0.12.0.dev1 lookupsid with explicit domain/guest and -no-pass, a reported domain SID and mixed user/group/alias rows. In particular, the DC computer account has a dollar suffix and SidTypeUser. This contradicts treating that type as a list of human users. The command's default RID bound cannot establish every directory account. Page 6 repeats the network operation to pipe names through grep/sed, losing domain/type context; the new skill instead keeps the original log and formats it offline. The prose also switches between user.txt and users.txt. The extracted list includes built-in, service and computer identities.

Page 6's password-spray output stops after the first displayed success, with later list entries unshown. That is evidence of the displayed successful pair, not evidence every account was tried or that all others failed. No spray was executed here. Page 7 uses --users and prints descriptions plus badpwdcount values, including a password-like description. Such metadata can be stale; a count is neither an account-policy decision nor a safe remaining-attempt budget. The new current-NetExec example requests one named user rather than assuming historical output or collecting the full domain.

Page 8 shows a different identity accessing DEV and retrieving a 601-byte PowerShell script. The source supplies its SMB password in the process arguments; the existing skill's prompt-based pattern avoids copying that approach. The page's final prose adds a trailing exclamation mark to the script credential that is absent from both the actual script and subsequent login command on page 9. That discrepancy is visible in the rendered source and was not normalized into a supposedly verified credential.

Page 9 constructs a PSCredential but never uses that variable in the shown backup action. Creating a credential object alone does not change the executing identity. An unconditional success-message statement after Compress-Archive is not independent evidence that an archive was created successfully. The following Evil-WinRM 3.5 transcript shows a prompt under the intended user's profile and a Ruby completion warning; the warning does not negate an established session. A profile path still does not replace an explicit account-SID check.

Page 10 displays SeBackupPrivilege and SeRestorePrivilege enabled in the current token, followed by successful native registry-save messages and downloads. The reusable lesson is to inspect that token and the API's backup conditions, rather than assuming arbitrary file reads or writes bypass permissions. Native registry export creates files and the hives contain sensitive material; no registry save, privilege activation, download or secret acquisition was attempted during ingestion.

Page 11 runs offline SAM/SYSTEM extraction, shows several records and explicitly fails to extract one account because it has no hash information. This is partial per-record output, not an all-or-nothing result or a complete domain credential database. The SAM store must retain its local-account scope; a DC hostname does not convert those records into NTDS results. The displayed Administrator record and the later Evil-WinRM profile prompt do not independently settle local-versus-domain identity without SID/token evidence. Long output values wrap across lines. Existing secretsdump and Evil-WinRM guidance already distinguishes the NT field from the whole record and preserves errors. No displayed password/hash was recovered, reused or tested.

**Primary verification, reviewed 2026-10-10.** [Impacket lookupsid source](https://github.com/fortra/impacket/blob/master/examples/lookupsid.py) verifies the positional exclusive RID bound, prompt/no-pass behavior, account-domain versus primary-domain SID selection, potential DC forwarding and logged exceptions swallowed at top level. Microsoft's [SID documentation](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows-server/identity/ad-ds/manage/understand-security-identifiers) establishes RID composition and local/domain identity scope. These support the new package's range and interpretation limits; they do not constitute a live RPC result.

NetExec's [SMB argument definitions](https://github.com/Pennyw0rth/NetExec/blob/main/nxc/protocols/smb/proto_args.py), [SMB dispatcher](https://github.com/Pennyw0rth/NetExec/blob/main/nxc/protocols/smb.py) and [SAMR user implementation](https://github.com/Pennyw0rth/NetExec/blob/main/nxc/protocols/smb/samruser.py) confirm named-user lookup before detail retrieval, broad enumeration when names are omitted and the AdminComment/BadPasswordCount fields. Errors can leave incomplete output; selected-name querying is not merely a display filter in the reviewed implementation. Microsoft's [badPwdCount documentation](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/adschema/a-badpwdcount) confirms nonreplication, per-DC maintenance and reset on successful authentication. No current domain policy or account state was queried.

The [Samba smbclient manual](https://www.samba.org/samba/docs/current/man-html/smbclient.1.html) verifies -U identity/password syntax, -N prompt suppression and default identity selection. Microsoft's [whoami reference](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows-server/administration/windows-commands/whoami), [file security and backup access](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/fileio/file-security-and-access-rights#backup-and-restore-access-rights) and [privilege constants](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/secauthz/privilege-constants) distinguish current token information, enabled backup privileges, backup-semantic API calls and read versus other access rights. A guessed backup-documentation URL was inaccessible; the accessible file-security and privilege references supplied the required evidence.

Relocated history: smbclient was previously marked documentation-reviewed 2026-10-09, Linux execution untested, citing Active pp. 3â€“6, BabyTwo pp. 3â€“8 and the Samba manual. Evil-WinRM was previously marked documentation-reviewed 2026-10-09, Linux execution untested, citing Administrator pp. 5, 8, 11, [upstream usage](https://github.com/Hackplayers/evil-winrm) and the [client implementation](https://github.com/Hackplayers/evil-winrm/blob/master/evil-winrm.rb). Those earlier source trails remain attributed here; no historical execution result was upgraded. Useful primary links remain in the independently installable skills.

**Checks actually run.** Focused validators passed for impacket-lookupsid, netexec, smbclient and evil-winrm. The repository checker passed for **157 packages, 197 Markdown files, 245 local links and 358 Bash blocks**; Bash was parsed only. Catalogue membership and git diff --check passed; the complete skill/catalogue diff was reviewed. All four affected CLI families were absent from PATH under their inspected entrypoint names. No CLI runtime fixture, LSA/SAMR/SMB/WinRM connection, Windows token or registry operation, source credential use, Linux execution or worker evaluation occurred. The ignored per-file work directory retains all-page extraction counts, eleven renders, before snapshots, relocated history, checks and the review diff.

Deliberately deferred material: target scanning/hosts changes, guest or password authentication, RID/user enumeration against a server, password spraying/reuse, remote file or hive acquisition, privilege activation, backup/registry export, credential extraction and pass-the-hash. Source-specific secrets, SID values and flag retrieval do not belong in portable guidance. No tool installation, system/configuration change, original-PDF deletion/move, commit or push occurred. Unrelated edits and methodologies were preserved.


### 41. Clicker.pdf

**Completed 2026-10-10.** All **15 physical PDF pages** were text-read, rendered and individually visually inspected. Screenshots on pages 4â€“9 supplied source code, request parameters, session context and execution output missing from extraction. Extraction produced 16,615 characters without warnings. Those dates are retained as a source inconsistency rather than silently corrected. SHA-256: `41469a4e4f7be49e0e24fd8e36b619b7b3efa5f48a1af2f0b71b8cc18e2d90ff`.

| Accepted change | Source pages and useful decision |
|---|---|
| New `showmount` | Page 2 motivates export advertisement interpretation, MOUNT-service/NFSv4 discovery limits and the distinction from file access or active sessions. |
| New `mount-nfs` | Pages 2â€“3 motivate a dedicated read-only mount, exact source/version/options verification, selected copying, identity/locking diagnosis and clean unmounting. |
| New `xml-pp` | Pages 12â€“14 motivate bounded trusted-input formatting, retaining original bytes, partial-output/error handling, mixed-content limitations and explicit entity-handling conditions. |
| Enriched `strace` | Pages 10â€“11 distinguish a denied credential transition and access check under ptrace from ordinary privileged behavior, successful reads or a useful zero exit status. |
| Enriched `php` | Pages 4â€“6 distinguish quoted values and bound WHERE data from untrusted dynamic SET identifiers/assignment fragments. |
| Enriched `sudo` | Pages 12â€“15 motivate child-specific environment review and guards that run only after retrieval/parsing. |
| Enriched `curl` proxy reference | Pages 13â€“14 expose the uppercase HTTP_PROXY prose versus lowercase http_proxy command distinction on Linux. Existing bypass/forwarding guidance remains in place. |

The catalogue now has 160 independent skills. No umbrella NFS or Perl exploitation package was added. Existing nmap, unzip, cp, find, strings, script, bash, mysql, ssh and ssh-keygen coverage already supports the remaining portable selection, source-scope and evidence decisions. Full exploit reproduction, web-shell construction and source-specific credential handling were deliberately excluded.

**Page coverage and source qualifications.** Page 1 supplies the lab synopsis, including NFS source acquisition, application mutations, a SUID helper and two sudo/environment paths. It describes the later nickname change loosely as a username change; page 4's actual query uses nickname. Page 2 performs a full TCP discovery stage and selected service follow-up, with output explicitly snipped. It does not establish UDP coverage. Its HTTP redirect provides a hostname lead. The subsequent hosts-file append is a system change and was not reproduced.

Page 2's showmount result advertises /mnt/backups to a wildcard client set. That does not reveal root-squash policy, security flavor or per-file rights. The printed mount overlays /mnt, uses nolock and does not request ro. The accepted skill instead requires a dedicated empty target, explicit inspection options and effective mount evidence. nolock is a locking choice, not an access-control bypass. Page 3 lists ZIP members and shows a Clicker web UI. A backup filename and matching-looking UI do not establish that all archived source equals the deployed application; the actual archive is not supplied for inspection or execution.

Page 4's screenshot shows save_profile constructing SET fragments from raw array keys while quoting values, then preparing the resulting SQL and binding the player in WHERE. Page 5 shows the save-and-close UI and an authenticated GET to save_game.php. Page 6 shows a modified parameter key, a redirect and a subsequently visible Administration option. These support a specific dynamic-identifier/authorization problem; calling prepare or quoting values does not protect text already interpolated as SQL structure. Proper field selection must also exclude columns the caller is not authorized to change. No SQL or application mutation was executed here.

Page 7 shows the admin player table and an export POST with threshold and extension. Page 8 shows exported text containing a four-click user although the preceding shown POST has a much higher threshold; those displays cannot establish an exact threshold predicate without the actual handler/request sequence. The narrative claims arbitrary extension acceptance, but the full export handler is not printed. The subsequent nickname request, PHP export request and page 9 output provide case-specific behavioral evidence. A form dropdown alone does not establish a server-side allowlist, and generating a file alone does not establish PHP execution. Page 9 separately shows id output in the generated page and a www-data callback transcript. These attack requests, exported payload, listener and callback were not reproduced.

Page 10 uses the correct util-linux script command/filename ordering, followed by interactive terminal changes. Existing script guidance already supports the relevant PTY distinctions; no terminal settings were changed here. Its find command suppresses permission diagnostics, so its SUID file list is visibility-limited. The custom helper's README names actions that create data, reset a password or delete users. Those are not harmless fuzz cases. The strings output contains fragmented path/command text interleaved with extra characters; it guides disassembly but does not alone establish a complete executable command or code path.

Page 11 traces action 4, which the preceding README says deletes users. The observed setreuid call fails with EPERM, then access(path, R_OK) fails with EACCES; the program prints a combined unreadable/not-found message and exits zero. The trace proves an attempted permission check in that context, not a successful read or absence of the file. Ptrace can suppress set-ID transitions, and access normally checks real rather than effective IDs. A working privileged run cannot be inferred from this failed traced run. Retrying the same action without tracing could have destructive effects. The later path-traversal command shows a snipped private-key block, not a complete local key artifact. No helper execution, fuzzing, traversal or key acquisition occurred.

Page 12 separately shows an SSH session and id for jack. Its sudo listing contains both a broad ALL entry and a specific root SETENV/NOPASSWD wrapper entry; the latter's password behavior must not be generalized to all commands. The wrapper checks EUID, assigns PATH, clears two Perl library variables and retrieves an HTTP response. A standalone set appears before the PATH assignment in the rendered script; that printed form is not an environment-clearing operation. It remains source text, not a validated deployed script.

Page 13 parses the response using xml_pp before checking NOSAVE, then otherwise saves the data. That guard cannot prevent retrieval or parsing. curl -s suppresses diagnostics and the displayed wrapper does not check transport/parser status; neither nonempty XML-looking output nor a later saved file establishes complete successful processing. The unquoted echo used for saving also need not preserve original response bytes. The XML field reporting a database check of OK is application-supplied data, not a separate database connection observed by this ingestion.

Page 13 prose names uppercase HTTP_PROXY while page 14's actual sudo invocation sets lowercase http_proxy. curl accepts the HTTP proxy environment variable only in lowercase on Linux; Windows environment-name behavior is different. Page 14 shows curl/7.81.0 through a Burp listener, a modified XML response with an external-entity declaration and a later root SSH id transcript. The proxy's displayed destination address differs from the earlier scan address, so those observations must keep their individual session context. The shown replacement-response body is distinct from the prose's reference to forwarding a modified request. No proxy, response mutation, entity expansion, root-key read or authentication was performed.

Page 15 describes an alternative Perl debugger environment path, a privileged permission change and a Bash -p session. Clearing PERL5LIB/PERLLIB alone does not cover all Perl startup controls; behavior also depends on the exact invocation and taint-mode rules. Existing bash guidance already explains that -p preserves an obtained effective identity rather than granting one from nothing. The source's permission mutation was not copied into a runnable skill example or executed.

**Primary verification, reviewed 2026-10-10.** The upstream-authored nfs-utils [showmount](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man8/showmount.8.html), [mount.nfs](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man8/mount.nfs.8.html), [NFS options](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man5/nfs.5.html) and [exports](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man5/exports.5.html) manuals verify MOUNT-service coverage, version negotiation, locking, hard/soft semantics and root-squash mapping. The [mount manual](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man8/mount.8.html) supports local mount state and generic inspection flags. These are documentation checks, not proof of this server's export policy.

The [strace manual](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man1/strace.1.html) verifies the %creds filter; Linux [execve](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/execve.2.html) and [access](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/access.2.html) manuals support the identity/ptrace and permission-check distinctions. PHP's [PDO prepare](https://www.php.net/manual/en/pdo.prepare.php) and [quote](https://www.php.net/manual/en/pdo.quote.php) documentation distinguish data values from identifiers and arbitrary syntax. The conclusion is static source review; no PDO driver or database fixture ran.

The maintainer's [xml_pp documentation](https://metacpan.org/dist/XML-Twig/view/tools/xml_pp/xml_pp), [CLI source](https://github.com/mirod/xmltwig/blob/master/tools/xml_pp/xml_pp) and [XML::Twig documentation](https://metacpan.org/pod/XML::Twig) verify load-before-print, streaming partial output, encoding/in-place modes, the mixed-content caveat and safe_parse exception handling. XML::Twig documents no_xxe as a constructor option; the reviewed CLI exposes no corresponding switch and does not set it. This does not establish the behavior of an unidentified installed distribution build, nor justify formatting untrusted entity-bearing input with elevated access.

The [sudoers manual](https://github.com/sudo-project/sudo/blob/main/docs/sudoers.man.in), [Perl startup/environment reference](https://perldoc.perl.org/perlrun) and [curl environment manual](https://curl.se/docs/manpage.html) verify the affected policy and child-program controls. A separate curl-book URL was unavailable; the accessible official curl manual explicitly documents lowercase-only http_proxy and the Windows caveat. No casing experiment on this Windows host was substituted for Linux validation.

Relocated history: strace was marked documentation-reviewed 2026-10-09, Linux execution untested, AdmirerToo p. 19, citing the [upstream manual source](https://github.com/strace/strace/blob/master/doc/strace.1.in). The curl proxy reference was marked documentation-reviewed 2026-10-09, Linux execution untested, Bamboo pp. 2â€“5, citing the curl manual and the [reviewed Squid scanner](https://gist.githubusercontent.com/xct/597d48456214b15108b2817660fdee00/raw/f1fff423d457655b0740699acf4a61126804d16f/squidscan.go). Its single-error-phrase classifier lesson remains portable runtime guidance; the case name/history lives here. Earlier verification was not upgraded to runtime success.

**Checks actually run.** Focused validators passed for showmount, mount-nfs, xml-pp, strace, php, sudo and curl. The repository checker passed for **160 packages, 200 Markdown files, 248 local links and 362 Bash blocks**; Bash was parsed only. Catalogue membership and git diff --check passed, and all changed skill/reference/catalogue content was reviewed. showmount, mount.nfs, xml_pp, strace, php and perl were absent from the inspected PATH. No CLI runtime fixture, NFS mount/query, PHP/XML parser, tracing, privileged-wrapper, proxy/target, Linux or worker execution occurred. The ignored per-file directory retains fifteen renders, extraction/page counts, before snapshots, relocated history, validation evidence and the review diff.

No tools were installed, original PDFs moved/deleted, system configuration changed, or commits/pushes made. Unrelated edits and methodologies were preserved.

The catalogue now contains 161 independent skills. Existing nmap, sqlite3, hashcat, sudo, bash, ssh and ssh-keygen coverage supports the remaining portable decisions; no duplicate wrapper/jail skill was created. Earlier jq/tar history was moved out of runtime footers without changing its original validation claims.

**Page coverage and source qualifications.** Page 1 supplies the lab synopsis. Page 2 shows Nmap 7.94SVN default-port TCP scanning with two open ports and 998 closed ports, not exhaustive TCP/UDP coverage. The hostname is introduced as likely and then put in a hosts file; that is an analyst-chosen mapping, not observed DNS evidence. The screenshot establishes editor, save, registration and login controls. No scanning, hosts change, account creation or application request was performed.

Page 3 shows a blocked import/system request and a source-specific object-introspection bypass. Its description calls import, write and open modules imprecisely. The available classes and imported modules depend on interpreter startup and process state; this is not a universally portable Python command. The source reports no frontend output and attributes it to capture behavior, but does not print the full web handler. The accepted guidance therefore makes that diagnosis conditional on inspecting the actual capture implementation. It does not claim to have verified this editor's internals.

Page 4 separately displays an app-production callback and subsequent shell/database discovery. The printed callback addresses differ between examples, so they are not silently treated as one session. Lack of job control describes the terminal context, not failed authentication. The database is a local SQLite file in the application instance directory; its actual bytes are not supplied for independent examination. No bypass, callback, shell or database access was executed here.

Page 5 shows SQLite 3.31.1 listing code/user tables and two application user records, followed by Hashcat 6.2.6 mode 0 recovery. A hash's printed shape alone does not establish MD5; the explicit mode and reported recoveries are source observations. The home-directory list is not a complete account inventory, and application identities/passwords are distinct from Linux logins. A later martin prompt provides separate session context, without a shown id command at that transition. Credentials and hashes were not copied into skills or used.

Page 6's sudo listing allows the wrapper as ALL users/groups with NOPASSWD; describing it only as a root-only policy would lose that scope. Root is the usual default invocation identity, not the only listed RunAs identity. The displayed old archive and JSON task do not prove that archive matches the currently deployed application. The task includes a hidden-file exclusion that is absent from the later task. The linked upstream program is vdbsh/backy; the Bash wrapper is local code, not automatically upstream behavior.

Page 7 shows argument/file checks, a jq transformation, an immediate rewrite of the input JSON, extraction of the archive list, an unquoted shell loop and a textual allowed-prefix test. The transformation runs before the prefix check despite the prose describing the checks in another order. The rewrite already changes state before the path decision; jq failure is not checked explicitly. Splitting raw output into shell words can differ from the original array. Only the archive list is filtered in the displayed wrapper, while other task fields are forwarded to a later backy invocation. Reopening a caller-writable path introduces a separate checked-versus-consumed-data question; the PDF does not demonstrate a race and this report does not claim one occurred.

Page 8 calls its overlapping literal path string an alternative encoding, but it is a one-pass replacement issue rather than a separate encoding. The runtime skill explains the same substitution property with harmless letters, without providing the traversal payload. The displayed backy 1.2 log reports no synchronization and then archiving; those are distinct stages. A tar warning about removing a path prefix affects stored names, not whether privileged source reads occurred. The accepted archive example uses an ordinary account and a separate empty destination instead of reproducing the privileged task.

Page 9 shows extracting the resulting compressed archive, then files owned by martin, including symlinks and hidden files. Extraction under that account explains restored ownership; it does not show changed ownership of the original root directory. Page 10 continues the listing, displays a snipped private key, and prints chmod/SSH commands. There is no resulting root prompt or id output on that page. The source claims successful root access, but the printed evidence directly establishes archive-content access and a proposed authentication step, not a verified root SSH session. No source archive/key artifact was available or used.

**Primary verification, reviewed 2026-10-10.** The [backy README](https://github.com/vdbsh/backy) documents its positional JSON input, fields and status mapping. The [upstream Go implementation](https://github.com/vdbsh/backy/blob/main/backy.go) was read, including task parsing, normalization, archive selection, subprocess output, rsync flags and rename handling. It reports version 1.2. The README's automatic corrupt-archive recovery claim exceeds the implementation's existence-only selection check. The skill follows the code and requires separate artifact verification. No upstream code was built or run. The repository is archived; installed identity/build still needs verification on Linux.

The [jq manual](https://jqlang.org/manual/) and [builtin definitions](https://github.com/jqlang/jq/blob/master/src/builtin.jq) support update assignment, map and non-overlapping substitution. The source's reduce over matches in the original input confirms that newly formed matches are not repeatedly consumed; the harmless once/twice result is documentation/source-derived, not an executed jq fixture. The Linux [path-resolution manual](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man7/path_resolution.7.html) supports the distinction between string operations and actual dot-component/symlink resolution.

Python's [os.system](https://docs.python.org/3/library/os.html#os.system), [subprocess.run](https://docs.python.org/3/library/subprocess.html#subprocess.run) and [redirect_stdout](https://docs.python.org/3/library/contextlib.html#contextlib.redirect_stdout) references support status encoding, output capture, text/buffer behavior and child-process boundaries. The added local fixture checks below corroborate the applicable Windows behavior; they do not validate the source web application or Linux wait-status encoding.

The authoritative GNU [tar manual](https://www.gnu.org/software/tar/manual/tar.html) and [options summary](https://www.gnu.org/s/tar/manual/html_section/All-Options.html) were accessible through indexed primary-source excerpts for member-name stripping and ordinary-user no-same-owner defaults. Direct manual/section fetches and a Savannah source fetch failed; this limitation is retained rather than claiming a full manual read. Indexed [Bash manual](https://www.gnu.org/s/bash/manual/bash.html) text confirms splitting after unquoted expansions. The PDF's full wrapper supplies the concrete data-flow example. No tar or Bash runtime path/ownership fixture was substituted from Windows for Linux semantics.

Relocated history: jq was documentation-reviewed 2026-10-09, Linux execution untested, for Backend pp. 2â€“4 and 12â€“15, BackendTwo pp. 11â€“16, and Bagel pp. 16 and 21â€“22, with the jq manual as source. These earlier reviews were not upgraded to runtime success.

**Page coverage and source qualifications.** Page 1 introduces the JavaScript editor, SQLite data and privileged backup workflow. Page 2 uses full TCP discovery followed by selected service detection; output is snipped and UDP is not covered. The shell pipeline turns human-readable scan lines into a port argument without showing an empty-result/error guard. Existing nmap guidance supplies the relevant coverage caution. The page also shows an application ZIP download and extraction. The ZIP itself is not supplied, so archive completeness and whether its source equals the deployed application cannot be independently established here.

Page 3 lists the extracted application files and a manifest pinning Flask, Flask-SQLAlchemy and js2py. That manifest is installation intent, not a live package inventory. Its registration screenshot and page 4's dashboard show one authenticated UI path. The printed /run_code handler reads JSON and passes its code value directly into js2py.eval_js; it does not itself show an authentication decorator or all surrounding middleware. The walkthrough's login sequence does not establish that the endpoint requires login. The sandbox claim is not established merely by using a JavaScript API inside Python; the actual reachable evaluation path and installed distribution/patch remain separate facts. Page 6 shows the modified editor content, a callback transcript, app's id result and a script PTY invocation. The reported id is evidence for that displayed process context. Page 7 shows a live-instance users.db listing and file identification, table names and two user records. The database header's last-writer version is not the running SQLite CLI version. The MD5 attribution initially comes from appearance, then the screenshot's lookup result; appearance alone is ambiguous.
